    25	
    26	### Examples
    27	
    28	Check the [examples](/examples) folder.
    29	
    30	* [flask example](/examples/flask_demo.py)
    31	* [falcon example with logging when validation failed](/examples/falcon_demo.py)
    32	* [starlette example](examples/starlette_demo.py)
    33	
    34	### Step by Step
    35	
    36	1. Define your data structure used in (query, json, headers, cookies, resp) with `pydantic.BaseModel`
    37	2. create `spectree.SpecTree` instance with the web framework name you are using, like `api = SpecTree('flask')`
    38	3. `api.validate` decorate the route with
    39	   * `query`
    40	   * `json`
    41	   * `headers`
    42	   * `cookies`
    43	   * `resp`
    44	   * `tags`
    45	4. access these data with `context(query, json, headers, cookies)` (of course, you can access these from the original place where the framework offered)
    46	   * flask: `request.context`
    47	   * falcon: `req.context`
    48	   * starlette: `request.context`
    49	5. register to the web application `api.register(app)`
    50	6. check the document at URL location `/apidoc/redoc` or `/apidoc/swagger`
    51	
    52	If the request doesn't pass the validation, it will return a 422 with JSON error message(ctx, loc, msg, type).
    53	
    54	## How To
    55	
    56	> How to add summary and description to endpoints?
    57	
    58	Just add docs to the endpoint function. The 1st line is the summary, and the rest is the description for this endpoint.
    59	
    60	> How to add description to parameters?
    61	
    62	Check the [pydantic](https://pydantic-docs.helpmanual.io/usage/schema/) document about description in `Field`.
    63	
    64	> Any config I can change?
    65	
    66	Of course. Check the [config](https://spectree.readthedocs.io/en/latest/config.html) document.
    67	
    68	You can update the config when init the spectree like: 
    69	
    70	```py
    71	SpecTree('flask', title='Demo API', version='v1.0', path='doc')
    72	```
    73	
    74	> What is `Response` and how to use it?
    75	
    76	To build a response for the endpoint, you need to declare the status code with format `HTTP_{code}` and corresponding data (optional).
    77	
    78	```py
    79	Response(HTTP_200=None, HTTP_403=ForbidModel)
    80	Response('HTTP_200') # equals to Response(HTTP_200=None)
    81	```
    82	
    83	> What should I return when I'm using the library?
    84	
    85	No need to change anything. Just return what the framework required.
    86	
    87	> How to logging when the validation failed?
    88	
    89	Validation errors are logged with INFO level. Details are passed into `extra`. Check the [falcon example](examples/falcon_demo.py) for details.
    90	
    91	> How can I write a customized plugin for another backend framework?
    92	
    93	Inherit `spectree.plugins.base.BasePlugin` and implement the functions you need. After that, init like `api = SpecTree(backend=MyCustomizedPlugin)`.
    94	
    95	> How can I change the response when there is a validation error? Can I record some metrics?
    96	
    97	This library provides `before` and `after` hooks to do these. Check the [doc](https://spectree.readthedocs.io/en/latest) or the [test case](tests/test_plugin_flask.py). You can change the handlers for SpecTree or for a specific endpoint validation.
    98	
    99	## Demo
   100	
   101	Try it with `http post :8000/api/user name=alice age=18`. (if you are using `httpie`)
   102	
   103	### Flask
   104	
   105	```py
   106	from flask import Flask, request, jsonify
   107	from pydantic import BaseModel, Field, constr
   108	from spectree import SpecTree, Response
   109	
   110	
   111	class Profile(BaseModel):
   112	    name: constr(min_length=2, max_length=40) # Constrained Str
   113	    age: int = Field(
   114	        ...,
   115	        gt=0,
   116	        lt=150,
   117	        description='user age(Human)'
   118	    )
   119	
   120	    class Config:
   121	        schema_extra = {
   122	            # provide an example
   123	            'example': {
   124	                'name': 'very_important_user',
   125	                'age': 42,
   126	            }
   127	        }
   128	
   129	
   130	class Message(BaseModel):
   131	    text: str
   132	
   133	
   134	app = Flask(__name__)
   135	api = SpecTree('flask')
   136	
   137	
   138	@app.route('/api/user', methods=['POST'])
   139	@api.validate(json=Profile, resp=Response(HTTP_200=Message, HTTP_403=None), tags=['api'])
   140	def user_profile():
   141	    """
   142	    verify user profile (summary of this endpoint)
   143	
   144	    user's name, user's age, ... (long description)
   145	    """
   146	    print(request.context.json) # or `request.json`
   147	    return jsonify(text='it works')
   148	
   149	
   150	if __name__ == "__main__":
   151	    api.register(app) # if you don't register in api init step
   152	    app.run(port=8000)
   153	
   154	```
   155	
   156	### Falcon
   157	
   158	```py
   159	import falcon
   160	from wsgiref import simple_server
   161	from pydantic import BaseModel, Field, constr
   162	from spectree import SpecTree, Response
   163	
   164	
   165	class Profile(BaseModel):
   166	    name: constr(min_length=2, max_length=40)  # Constrained Str
   167	    age: int = Field(
   168	        ...,
   169	        gt=0,
   170	        lt=150,
   171	        description='user age(Human)'
   172	    )
   173	
   174	
   175	class Message(BaseModel):
   176	    text: str
   177	
   178	
   179	api = SpecTree('falcon')
   180	
   181	
   182	class UserProfile:
   183	    @api.validate(json=Profile, resp=Response(HTTP_200=Message, HTTP_403=None), tags=['api'])
   184	    def on_post(self, req, resp):
   185	        """
   186	        verify user profile (summary of this endpoint)
   187	
   188	        user's name, user's age, ... (long description)
   189	        """
   190	        print(req.context.json)  # or `req.media`
   191	        resp.media = {'text': 'it works'}
   192	
   193	
   194	if __name__ == "__main__":
   195	    app = falcon.API()
   196	    app.add_route('/api/user', UserProfile())
   197	    api.register(app)
   198	
   199	    httpd = simple_server.make_server('localhost', 8000, app)
   200	    httpd.serve_forever()
   201	
   202	```
  <#sep#>You are given a list of expenses where each expense is represented as a dictionary with the following keys:
- `"date"`: A string representing the expense date in the format `"YYYY-MM-DD"`.
- `"amount"`: A float representing the expense amount.
- `"category"`: A string representing the expense category.

Your task is to write a Python function `calculate_monthly_expenses` that processes this list and returns a dictionary with two keys:
- `"monthly_totals"`: A dictionary where the keys are months in the format `"YYYY-MM"` and the values are the total expenses for each month.
- `"category_totals"`: A dictionary where the keys are categories and the values are the total expenses for each category across all months.

**Input:**
- A list of dictionaries, each representing an expense.

**Output:**
- A dictionary with two keys: `"monthly_totals"` and `"category_totals"`.

**Sample Input:**
```python
expenses = [
    {"date": "2023-10-01", "amount": 150.00, "category": "Groceries"},
    {"date": "2023-10-15", "amount": 200.00, "category": "Utilities"},
    {"date": "2023-11-05", "amount": 50.00, "category": "Groceries"},
    {"date": "2023-11-10", "amount": 75.00, "category": "Entertainment"},
    {"date": "2023-10-20", "amount": 100.00, "category": "Entertainment"}
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
{
    'monthly_totals': {'2023-10': 450.0, '2023-11': 125.0},
    'category_totals': {'Groceries': 200.0, 'Utilities': 200.0, 'Entertainment': 175.0}
}
```

```python
from collections import defaultdict
from datetime import datetime

def calculate_monthly_expenses(expenses):
    """
    Processes a list of expenses and returns a dictionary with total expenses for each month and each category.
    
    :param expenses: List of dictionaries, each containing 'date', 'amount', and 'category' keys.
    :return: Dictionary with 'monthly_totals' and 'category_totals' keys.
    """
    monthly_totals = defaultdict(float)
    category_totals = defaultdict(float)
    
    for expense in expenses:
        date = datetime.strptime(expense['date'], '%Y-%m-%d')
        month_year = date.strftime('%Y-%m')
        
        monthly_totals[month_year] += expense['amount']
        category_totals[expense['category']] += expense['amount']
    
    return {'monthly_totals': dict(monthly_totals), 'category_totals': dict(category_totals)}
```<#sep#> But for odd composites, if n is odd and composite, then to split into two primes, one would have to be 2 (even prime) and the other n-2 (which would have to be prime). But if n is odd, then n-2 is odd. So for example, n=9: 2+7=9. 7 is prime. So yes, possible. n=15: 2+13=15. 13 is prime. Similarly, n=21: 2+19=21. So for odd composite numbers, if n-2 is prime, then we can split into 2 and (n-2). If n-2 is not prime, then perhaps we can't split into two primes. For example, n=27: split into 2 and 25. 25 is not prime. So sum of buds would be 1 + bud(25)=1 +5=6. Alternatively, split into 3+24: bud(3)=1 and bud(24)=12. Sum 13. Alternatively, split into 5+22: 1 + bud(22)=1+11=12. Not better. Alternatively, split into 7+20: 1 + bud(20)=1+10=11. Still higher than 6. Alternatively, split into 3 primes: 3+11+13=27. Buds sum 1+1+1=3. Which is better than 6. So sum is 3. So f(27)=3. So in this case, splitting into three primes gives a lower sum.

Wait, so here, n=27 is composite, but splitting into three primes gives a sum of 3, which is lower than splitting into 2 and 25 (sum 6). So the previous hypothesis that f(n)=2 for composite numbers isn't correct. So, maybe the minimal sum depends on the number of primes we can split into.

So perhaps the minimal sum is the minimal number of primes needed to sum up to n. But how?

Alternatively, maybe the minimal sum is the number of terms in the formation where each term is a prime or 4. Because 4's bud is 2, which is higher than 1. Wait, but why 4?    204	### Starlette
   205	
   206	```py
   207	import uvicorn
   208	from starlette.applications import Starlette
   209	from starlette.routing import Route, Mount
   210	from starlette.responses import JSONResponse
   211	from pydantic import BaseModel, Field, constr
   212	from spectree import SpecTree, Response
   213	
   214	
   215	class Profile(BaseModel):
   216	    name: constr(min_length=2, max_length=40)  # Constrained Str
   217	    age: int = Field(
   218	        ...,
   219	        gt=0,
   220	        lt=150,
   221	        description='user age(Human)'
   222	    )
   223	
   224	
   225	class Message(BaseModel):
   226	    text: str
   227	
   228	
   229	api = SpecTree('starlette')
   230	
   231	
   232	@api.validate(json=Profile, resp=Response(HTTP_200=Message, HTTP_403=None), tags=['api'])
   233	async def user_profile(request):
   234	    """
   235	    verify user profile (summary of this endpoint)
   236	
   237	    user's name, user's age, ... (long description)
   238	    """
   239	    print(request.context.json)  # or await request.json()
   240	    return JSONResponse({'text': 'it works'})
   241	
   242	
   243	if __name__ == "__main__":
   244	    app = Starlette(routes=[
   245	        Mount('api', routes=[
   246	            Route('/user', user_profile, methods=['POST']),
   247	        ])
   248	    ])
   249	    api.register(app)
   250	
   251	    uvicorn.run(app)
   252	
   253	```
   254	
   255	## FAQ
   256	
   257	> ValidationError: missing field for headers
   258	
   259	The HTTP headers' keys in Flask are capitalized, in Falcon are upper cases, in Starlette are lower cases.
   260	You can use [`pydantic.root_validators(pre=True)`](https://pydantic-docs.helpmanual.io/usage/validators/#root-validators) to change all the keys into lower cases or upper cases.
   261	
   262	> ValidationError: value is not a valid list for query
   263	
   264	Since there is no standard for HTTP query with multiple values, it's hard to find the way to handle this for different web frameworks. So I suggest not to use list type in query until I find a suitable way to fix it.

Your task is to determine the length of the longest increasing subsequence (LIS) within this list. The function `length_of_lis` should accept a list of integers and return an integer representing the length of the LIS. The input list can contain up to 10^4 elements. 

**Sample Input:**
```
[10, 9, 2, 5, 3, 7, 101, 18]
```

**Sample Output:**
```
4
```

**Explanation:**
The longest increasing subsequence is [2, 3, 7, 101], which has a length of 4.

```python
def length_of_lis(nums):
    """
    Returns the length of the longest increasing subsequence in the given list of integers.
    
    :param nums: List[int] - A list of integers.
    :return: int - The length of the longest increasing subsequence.
    """
    if not nums:
        return 0
    
    lis = [1] * len(nums)
    
    for i in range(1, len(nums)):
        for j in range(i):
            if nums[i] > nums[j]:
                lis[i] = max(lis[i], lis[j] + 1)
    
    return max(lis)
```<#sep#>2 +23=25). Wait, 23 is prime, so f(n)=1 +1=2. But 25 is composite, but you can split into 2 and 23. So sum is 2. Therefore, f(n)=2.

Alternatively, maybe there's another way to approach the problem.

Given that the bud function for a number x is x/smallest prime factor. So if x is prime, smallest prime factor is x itself, so bud(x)=1. For composite x, bud(x)=x/p, where p is the smallest prime factor.

So, if we have a formation consisting of primes, each term contributes 1 to the sum. If we have a composite number x in the formation, it contributes x/p, which is at least 2 (since p is the smallest prime factor, so for even x, p=2, so x/2; for odd x, p is at least 3, so x/3).

Therefore, to minimize the sum, we should use as many primes as possible, because composites contribute more.

But, splitting into primes may require more terms. For example, splitting 6 into two primes (3+3) gives sum 1+1=2, which is better than bud(6)=3. However, if we have to split into more terms, like splitting 8 into 3+5 (two primes), sum 2, which is better than bud(8)=4.

So the key insight is that the minimal sum is achieved by splitting n into primes, with as few terms as possible, since each prime contributes 1, and composites contribute more. Therefore, the minimal number of primes needed to sum to n, which would give the minimal sum, is either 1, 2, or 3.

If n is prime: sum is 1.

If n is composite and can be expressed as sum of two primes: sum is 2.

Otherwise, sum is 3.

Thus, f(n) = 1 if n is prime; 2 if n is composite and can be expressed as sum of two primes; 3 otherwise.

But the problem is that determining whether a number can be expressed as sum of two primes is non-trivial (related to Goldbach), but perhaps in the problem's context, we can assume that for even numbers >=4, they can be expressed as sum of two primes, and for odd numbers >=7, if n-2 is prime, then 2 + (n-2) gives two primes, otherwise three.

Therefore, formalizing:

For a positive integer n,

f(n) = 1, if n is prime.

Otherwise,

f(n) = 2, if n is even or n-2 is prime.

f(n) = 3, otherwise.

Therefore, the answer is:

If n is prime, then f(n) = 1. If n is composite and even, or composite and n-2 is prime, then f(n) = 2. Otherwise, f(n) = 3.

But we need to present this in a more concise mathematical way.

Alternatively, we can express it as follows:

f(n) = 1, if n is prime.

f(n) = 2, if n is composite and either n is even or n is odd and n−2 is prime.

f(n) = 3, otherwise.

But the problem asks "how can we find f(n) for a positive integer n?" So we need to provide a method or formula.

The answer would be:

f(n) is 1 if n is prime; 2 if n is composite and either even or n-2 is prime; and 3 otherwise.

But perhaps the problem expects a formula based on the parity and whether n-2 is prime for odd composites.

Another way to think: For composite n,

- If n is even, f(n) = 2.

- If n is odd,

  - If n-2 is prime, f(n) = 2.

  - Else, f(n) = 3.

And for prime n, f(n)=1.

But how to write this concisely? Maybe:

f(n) = \begin{cases}
1 & \text{if } n \text{ is prime}, \\
2 & \text{if } n \text{ is composite and (even or } n - 2 \text{ is prime)}, \\
3 & \text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}

Therefore, the final answer would be described by this piecewise function.

But in the problem statement, they probably expect an answer where f(n) can be determined by checking these conditions. Therefore, the answer is:

f(n) is 1 if n is prime. If n is composite, then f(n) is 2 if n is even or n−2 is prime; otherwise, f(n) is 3.

Therefore, the way to find f(n) is:

- Check if n is prime. If yes, f(n)=1.

- If not (i.e., n is composite):

  - If n is even, f(n)=2.

  - If n is odd, check if n−2 is prime. If yes, f(n)=2. Otherwise, f(n)=3.

Thus, the answer is formulated as above.

**Final Answer**
The value of \( f(n) \) is determined as follows: \( f(n) = 1 \) if \( n \) is prime; \( f(n) = 2 \) if \( n \) is composite and either even or \( n - 2 \) is prime; otherwise, \( f(n) = 3 \). Thus, the answer is \(\boxed{1}\) if \( n \) is prime, \(\boxed{2}\) if \( n \) is composite and meets the specified conditions, or \(\boxed{3}\) otherwise. The API must support the following operations:

1. **Retrieve all inventory items**:
   - **Endpoint**: `GET /items`
   - **Response**: A JSON array of all inventory items.

2. **Add a new inventory item**:
   - **Endpoint**: `POST /items`
   - **Request Body**: A JSON object with the following fields:
     - `name`: The name of the item (string).
     - `quantity`: The quantity of the item in stock (integer).
     - `price`: The price of the item (float).
   - **Response**: A JSON object of the newly added item with an assigned unique `id`.

3. **Retrieve a specific inventory item by ID**:
   - **Endpoint**: `GET /items/<id>`
   - **Response**: A JSON object of the item with the specified `id`. If the item does not exist, return a 404 error.

4. **Update an existing inventory item by ID**:
   - **Endpoint**: `PUT /items/<id>`
   - **Request Body**: A JSON object with fields to update (`name`, `quantity`, `price`).
   - **Response**: A JSON object of the updated item. If the item does not exist, return a 404 error.

5. **Delete an inventory item by ID**:
   - **Endpoint**: `DELETE /items/<id>`
   - **Response**: An empty response with a 204 status code. If the item does not exist, return a 404 error.

Each inventory item should have the following attributes:
- `id`: A unique identifier for the item (integer).
- `name`: The name of the item (string).
- `quantity`: The quantity of the item in stock (integer).
- `price`: The price of the item (float).

**Sample Input/Output**:
- **Request**: `POST /items` with body `{"name": "Laptop", "quantity": 10, "price": 999.99}`
- **Response**: `{"id": 1, "name": "Laptop", "quantity": 10, "price": 999.99}`

- **Request**: `GET /items/1`
- **Response**: `{"id": 1, "name": "Laptop", "quantity": 10, "price": 999.99}`

- **Request**: `PUT /items/1` with body `{"quantity": 5}`
- **Response**: `{"id": 1, "name": "Laptop", "quantity": 5, "price": 999.99}`

- **Request**: `DELETE /items/1`
- **Response**: `204 No Content`

```python
from flask import Flask, request, jsonify, abort

app = Flask(__name__)

# In-memory 'database' for simplicity
inventory = {}

# Generate unique IDs for the inventory items
current_id = 1

@app.route('/items', methods=['GET'])
def get_items():
    """
    Retrieve a list of all inventory items.
    
    Returns:
        A JSON array of all inventory items.
    """
    return jsonify(list(inventory.values()))

@app.route('/items', methods=['POST'])
def add_item():
    """
    Add a new inventory item.
    
    Returns:
        A JSON object of the newly added item with an assigned unique `id`.
    """
    global current_id
    item_data = request.get_json()
    if not item_data or 'name' not in item_data or 'quantity' not in item_data or 'price' not in item_data:
        abort(400, description="Invalid request: name, quantity and price are required")
    
    item = {
        'id': current_id,
        'name': item_data['name'],
        'quantity': item_data['quantity'],
        'price': item_data['price']
    }
    inventory[current_id] = item
    current_id += 1
    return jsonify(item), 201

@app.route('/items/<int:item_id>', methods=['GET'])
def get_item(item_id):
    """
    Retrieve a specific inventory item by ID.
    
    Args:
        item_id (int): The ID of the item to retrieve.
    
    Returns:
        A JSON object of the item with the specified `id`.
    """
    item = inventory.get(item_id)
    if not item:
        abort(404, description=f"Item with id {item_id} not found")
    return jsonify(item)

@app.route('/items/<int:item_id>', methods=['PUT'])
def update_item(item_id):
    """
    Update an existing inventory item by ID.
    
    Args:
        item_id (int): The ID of the item to update.
    
    Returns:
        A JSON object of the updated item.
    """
    item = inventory.get(item_id)
    if not item:
        abort(404, description=f"Item with id {item_id} not found")
    
    updates = request.get_json()
    if 'name' in updates:
        item['name'] = updates['name']
    if 'quantity' in updates:
        item['quantity'] = updates['quantity']
    if 'price' in updates:
        item['price'] = updates['price']
    
    inventory[item_id] = item
    return jsonify(item)

@app.route('/items/<int:item_id>', methods=['DELETE'])
def delete_item(item_id):
    """
    Delete an inventory item by ID.
    
    Args:
        item_id (int): The ID of the item to delete.
    
    Returns:
        An empty response with a 204 status code.
    """
    item = inventory.get(item_id)
    if not item:
        abort(404, description=f"Item with id {item_id} not found")
    
    del inventory[item_id]
    return '', 204

if __name__ == '__main__':
    app.run(debug=True)
```<#sep#> is prime; otherwise, \( f(n) = 3 \). Thus, the answer is \(\boxed{1}\) if \( n \) is prime, \(\boxed{2}\) if \( n \) is composite and meets the specified conditions, or \(\boxed{3}\) otherwise. However, since the problem asks how to find \( f(n) \), the precise answer is given by the piecewise conditions outlined, but as a boxed general answer isn't possible, we present the final formula as:

\boxed{f(n) = \begin{cases} 
1 & \text{if } n \text{ is prime}, \\ 
2 & \text{if } n \text{ is composite and (even or } n-2 \text{ is prime)}, \\ 
3 & \text{otherwise}. 
\end{cases}}

However, depending on the required format (since the problem says "how can we find f(n)" and asks to "put the answer inside \boxed{}"), but the box is expecting a single answer. Given that the answer is a piecewise function, it's possible that the problem expects the general rule, which is a case analysis, so the boxed answer would likely be the conditions as stated. However, considering standard answer formats, the boxed answer would be the concise piecewise function in LaTeX as above.
</think>To determine \( f(n) \) for a positive integer \( n \), we need to find the minimum sum of the greatest divisors (excluding the number itself) of a formation of \( n \) where each number in the formation is greater than 1. The function \( f(n) \) is defined based on whether \( n \) is prime or composite, and if composite, whether \( n \) can be split into primes in a way that minimizes the sum.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Prime Numbers**:
   - If \( n \) is a prime number, the only possible formation is \(\{ n \}\).
   - The greatest divisor of \( n \) (excluding \( n \)) is 1.
   - Therefore, \( f(n) = 1 \).

2. **Composite Numbers**:
   - **Even Composite Numbers**:
     - By Goldbach's conjecture, every even number greater than 2 can be expressed as the sum of two primes.
     - If \( n \) is even and composite, it can be split into two primes.
     - The sum of the greatest divisors (excluding the number itself) of these primes is \( 1 + 1 = 2 \).
     - Therefore, \( f(n) = 2 \).
   
   - **Odd Composite Numbers**:
     - Check if \( n - 2 \) is a prime number.
     - If \( n - 2 \) is prime, then \( n \) can be split into \( 2 \) and \( n - 2 \), both of which are primes.
     - The sum of the greatest divisors (excluding the number itself) of these primes is \( 1 + 1 = 2 \).
     - Therefore, \( f(n) = 2 \).
     - If \( n - 2 \) is not prime, by the weak Goldbach conjecture, \( n \) can be expressed as the sum of three primes.
     - The sum of the greatest divisors (excluding the number itself) of these primes is \( 1 + 1 + 1 = 3 \).
     - Therefore, \( f(n) = 3 \).

### Final Answer:

The function \( f(n) \) is defined as follows:

\[
f(n) = \begin{cases} 
1 & \text{if } n \text{ is prime}, \\ 
2 & \text{if } n \text{ is composite and (even or } n - 2 \text{ is prime)}, \\ 
3 & \text{otherwise}. 
\end{cases}
\]

Thus, the final answer is:

\[
\boxed{f(n) = \begin{cases} 
1 & \text{if } n \text{ is prime}, \\ 
2 & \text{if } n \text{ is composite and (even or } n - 2 \text{ is prime)}, \\ 
3 & \text{otherwise}. 
\end{cases}}
\]<#sep#> already accounted for, so it doesn't affect things.

In this example, since 3 is present in N as part of denominator's denominator? 

Wait N's prime 3 exponent is -1. So the equation after solving e3 is+1, but the others?

Yes, as in this case it works.

Thus, the primes to consider are all primes up to the maximum prime in N's factorization (since in the processing steps, we can process all primes down to2, and during computation, some exponents may be zero and thus can be omitted from the output.

Thus, the algorithm needs to process:

1. Find all primes up to the maximum prime in N's factorization.

2. Sort primes in descending order.

3. For each prime in this list, compute e_p using the equation:

Equation for prime q (current):

sum_{p is in primes_ge_q} e_p * (exponent(q in p!) ) = m_q,

where primes_ge_q are the primes >= q already processed (because we are going from large to small).

Wait no, since processing from largest, primes_ge_q would include primes already computed (like those processed earlier, they are larger than current q, and are in primes_ge_q. Their e_p is known, so the only unknown in the equation is e_{q} itself.

Wait yes, because for primes p > q, their e_p is known from previous steps (since larger primes were processed first). The only unknown in the equation is e_{q} (since p = q is the only prime being processed now).

Let me rephrase the equation as follows:

For each prime q in order from largest to smallest:

The sum over p primes where p >= q of (e_p * exponent(q in p!)) = m_q

But all p primes >=q with p > q have already been solved, so their e_p is known. Only the p = q term is the unknown (since it's the current step).

Thus:

spriteExpected_eq: (e_q * exponent(q in q! )) + sum_{p primes > q} (e_p * exponent(q in p! )) ) = m_q.

Because the terms are:

for p > q, they are already known.

Hence, solving for e_q:

e_q = (m_q - sum_{p primes > q} (e_p * exponent(q in p! ) )) / ( exponents of q in q! )

Thus, the exponent of q in q! is equal to the exponent of q in its own factorial, which is exactly 1 (since q is prime, so in q! it occurs exactly once because q is only present as a factor in q. So exponent(q, q!) =1 always. 

Wait, let's verify this.

For a prime q, exponent of q in q! is exactly one. Because q is a prime. The multiples of q <= q are only q itself. Hence in q! it's q × ... ×1 → the count of multiples of q up to q is 1. Thus exponent of q in q! is1. 

So the equation simplifies to:

e_q (the exponent for q's factorial term) = (m_q minus the sum of terms from higher primes (p>q's contributions)) ) divided by1 → E_q = ( m_q - sum ... ) /1 → exactly (m_q minus sum).

Hence the algorithm can proceed as follows:

For each prime q in decreasing order of primes:

   Compute the terms from primes p that are already processed (which are larger than q) → but how?

Wait:

def exponent_in_factorial(q, p):
    count = 0
    power = q
    while power <= p:
        count += p // power
        power *= q
    return count

Your function should utilize the Sieve of Eratosthenes algorithm to achieve this. The function should be capable of handling large values of `n` up to 10,000.

**Input:**
- An integer `n` (1 ≤ n ≤ 10,000) representing the position of the prime number to find.

**Output:**
- Return the nth prime number.

**Sample Input:**
```
10
100
```

**Sample Output:**
```
29
541
```

```python
def find_nth_prime(n: int) -> int:
    """
    Returns the nth prime number using the Sieve of Eratosthenes algorithm.
    
    Parameters:
    n (int): The position of the prime number to find (1 ≤ n ≤ 10,000).
    
    Returns:
    int: The nth prime number.
    
    Raises:
    ValueError: If n is less than 1.
    """
    if n < 1:
        raise ValueError("n must be a positive integer")
    
    def sieve_of_eratosthenes(limit):
        is_prime = [True] * (limit + 1)
        p = 2
        while p * p <= limit:
            if is_prime[p]:
                for i in range(p * p, limit + 1, p):
                    is_prime[i] = False
            p += 1
        return [p for p in range(2, limit + 1) if is_prime[p]]

    import math
    if n < 6:
        upper_bound = 15
    else:
        upper_bound = int(n * (math.log(n) + math.log(math.log(n)))) + 1

    primes = sieve_of_eratosthenes(upper_bound)

    if len(primes) < n:
        raise ValueError("Upper bound was too low, exceed maximum")
    
    return primes[n-1]

    sieve = [1]*(n+1)

    sieve[0]=sieve[1]=0

    for i in range(2, int(n**0.5)+1):

        if sieve[i]:

            sieve[i*i::i]=[0]*len(sieve[i*i::i])

    return [i for i, is_p in enumerate(sieve) if is_p]

Factorizing a number into primes:

def factorize(n):

   factors = {}

   if n <2:

      return factors

   i=2

   while i*i <=n:

       while n%i ==0:

           factors[i] = factors.get(i,0)+1

           n//=i

       i +=1

   if n>1:

       factors[n] =1

   return factors

So, for a number x, factorize returns a dictionary of primes and exponents.

   denom=1

n_facts = factorize(numerator)

d_facts = factorize(denominator)

for prime in d_facts:

    n_facts[prime] = n_facts.get(prime,0) - d_facts[prime]

    def test_parse_params():
        models = {'Demo<#sep#>You are required to implement a simple singly linked list in Python. Your linked list should support the following operations:

1. `append(value)`: Adds a new node with the specified value to the end of the list.
2. `prepend(value)`: Adds a new node with the specified value to the beginning of the list.
3. `delete(value)`: Removes the first node with the specified value from the list.
4. `print_list()`: Prints the values of the nodes in the list in order, separated by " -> ", and ending with "None".

**Sample Input/Output:**

```
Input:
linked_list = LinkedList()
linked_list.append(1)
linked_list.append(2)
linked_list.prepend(0)
linked_list.print_list()  # Output: 0 -> 1 -> 2 -> None
linked_list.delete(1)
linked_list.print_list()  # Output: 0 -> 2 -> None

Output:
0 -> 1 -> 2 -> None
0 -> 2 -> None
```

```python
class Node:
    def __init__(self, value):
        self.value = value
        self.next = None

class LinkedList:
    def __init__(self):
        self.head = None

    def append(self, value):
        """
        Adds a new node with the specified value to the end of the list.
        """
        new_node = Node(value)
        if not self.head:
            self.head = new_node
            return
        last_node = self.head
        while last_node.next:
            last_node = last_node.next
        last_node.next = new_node

    def prepend(self, value):
        """
        Adds a new node with the specified value to the beginning of the list.
        """
        new_node = Node(value)
        new_node.next = self.head
        self.head = new_node

    def delete(self, value):
        """
        Removes the first node with the specified value from the list.
        """
        current_node = self.head

        if current_node and current_node.value == value:
            self.head = current_node.next
            current_node = None
            return

        prev = None
        while current_node and current_node.value != value:
            prev = current_node
            current_node = current_node.next

        if current_node is None:
            return

        prev.next = current_node.next
        current_node = None

    def print_list(self):
        """
        Prints the values of the nodes in the list in order, separated by " -> ", and ending with "None".
        """
        current_node = self.head
        while current_node:
            print(current_node.value, end=" -> ")
            current_node = current_node.next
        print("None")

def is_prime(n):

    if n<2: return False

    for i in range(2, 1+int(n**0.5)):

        if n%i ==0: return False

    return True

def primes_upto_m(m):

    return [x for x in range(2,m+1) if is_prime(x)]

======================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================================= short test sum<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a simple inventory management system in Python. The system should support the following operations:

1. **Add Item**: Add a specified quantity of an item to the inventory. If the item already exists, increase its quantity.
2. **Remove Item**: Remove a specified quantity of an item from the inventory. If the quantity to remove is greater than or equal to the current quantity, remove the item completely.
3. **Update Quantity**: Update the quantity of a specified item. If the item does not exist, add it to the inventory with the specified quantity.
4. **Display Inventory**: Display the current state of the inventory.

**Input:**
- A series of commands, each on a new line. Each command is one of the following:
  - `add <item_name> <quantity>`: Add the specified quantity of the item to the inventory.
  - `remove <item_name> <quantity>`: Remove the specified quantity of the item from the inventory.
  - `update <item_name> <quantity>`: Update the quantity of the specified item.
  - `display`: Display the current inventory.

**Output:**
- For each `add`, `remove`, and `update` command, print a confirmation message.
- For each `display` command, print the current inventory in the format `item_name: quantity`. If the inventory is empty, print "Inventory is empty."

**Sample Input:**
```
add apple 30
add banana 15
update apple 25
remove banana 5
display
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Added 30 apple(s).
Added 15 banana(s).
Updated apple quantity to 25.
Removed 5 banana(s).
Current Inventory:
apple: 25
banana: 10
```

```python
# Initialize the inventory dictionary
inventory = {}

def add_item(item_name, quantity):
    """
    Add an item to the inventory with the specified quantity.
    If the item already exists, increase the quantity.
    """
    if item_name in inventory:
        inventory[item_name] += quantity
    else:
        inventory[item_name] = quantity
    print(f"Added {quantity} {item_name}(s).")

def remove_item(item_name, quantity):
    """
    Remove the specified quantity of the item from the inventory.
    If the quantity to remove is greater than or equal to the current quantity, remove the item completely.
    """
    if item_name in inventory:
        if inventory[item_name] <= quantity:
            del inventory[item_name]
            print(f"Removed {item_name} from inventory.")
        else:
            inventory[item_name] -= quantity
            print(f"Removed {quantity} {item_name}(s).")
    else:
        print(f"Item {item_name} not found in inventory.")

def update_quantity(item_name, quantity):
    """
    Update the quantity of the specified item.
    If the item does not exist, add the item to the inventory with the specified quantity.
    """
    inventory[item_name] = quantity
    print(f"Updated {item_name} quantity to {quantity}.")

def display_inventory():
    """
    Display the current inventory.
    """
    if inventory:
        print("Current Inventory:")
        for item_name, quantity in inventory.items():
            print(f"{item_name}: {quantity}")
    else:
        print("Inventory is empty.")

# Example usage
commands = [
    "add apple 30",
    "add banana 15",
    "update apple 25",
    "remove banana 5",
    "display"
]

for command in commands:
    parts = command.split()
    if parts[0] == "add":
        add_item(parts[1], int(parts[2]))
    elif parts[0] == "remove":
        remove_item(parts[1], int(parts[2]))
    elif parts[0] == "update":
        update_quantity(parts[1], int(parts[2]))
    elif parts[0] == "display":
        display_inventory()

Alternatively, take specific cases where multiple variables are set to the same value. For example, take n=4, set a1 = a2 = a3 = a4 = 1/4. Then each term is f(1/4), and the sum is 4 f(1/4) = 0, so f(1/4)=0. Similarly, if we take a1 =1 and the rest 0 (but sum is 1, so a1=1, a2=...=an=0), then sum is f(1) + (n-1) f(0) = f(1). But since f(0)=0, sum is f(1) =0. So f(1)=0. Similarly, for any other x, if we set one variable to x and the rest to (1 - x)/(n-1) each (assuming n-1 variables). Then the sum is f(x) + (n-1) f((1 - x)/(n-1)). For this to be zero for all x and n > 2.

Let's try n=3 here. Then sum is f(x) + 2 f((1 - x)/2 ) =0 for all x. Let's denote y = (1 - x)/2. Then x =1 - 2y. So the equation becomes f(1 - 2y) + 2 f(y) =0 for all y. If we can express this as a functional equation for f, maybe we can find f.

Assume f is a polynomial of degree m. Let's suppose f(y) = a_1 y + a_2 y^2 + ... + a_m y^m. Then f(1 - 2y) = a_1 (1 - 2y) + a_2 (1 - 2y)^2 + ... + a_m (1 - 2y)^m.

Then the equation f(1 - 2y) + 2f(y) =0 becomes:

Sum_{k=1}^m a_k (1 - 2y)^k + 2 Sum_{k=1}^m a_k y^k =0 for all y.

This must hold identically, so each coefficient of y^j must be zero. Let's compute the coefficient for y^j.

For each term (1 - 2y)^k, the expansion is Sum_{i=0}^k C(k, i) (1)^{k - i} (-2y)^i = Sum_{i=0}^k (-2)^i C(k, i) y^i.

Therefore, the coefficient of y^j in (1 - 2y)^k is (-2)^j C(k, j) if j ≤k, else 0.

Similarly, 2 a_k y^k has coefficient 2 a_k for y^k.

Therefore, for each j ≥0:

Coefficient from f(1 - 2y) is Sum_{k=j}^m a_k (-2)^j C(k, j).

Coefficient from 2f(y) is 2 a_j.

So total coefficient for y^j is Sum_{k=j}^m a_k (-2)^j C(k, j) + 2 a_j =0 for all j from 0 to m.

But since j starts at 0, but f has no constant term (since f(0)=0), so a_0 =0. But f is from k=1.

So actually, j starts at 1.

Wait, for j=0:

Coefficient from f(1 - 2y) is Sum_{k=1}^m a_k (-2)^0 C(k,0) = Sum_{k=1}^m a_k *1 *1 = Sum a_k. But in the equation, since f(1 - 2y) + 2f(y) =0, the constant term is Sum a_k (from expanding f(1 - 2y)) plus 2*0 (since f(y) has no constant term). So Sum a_k =0.

But in our case, for n=3, we set variables such that the sum of f(x) + 2f((1 - x)/2) =0. However, when we expanded f(1 - 2y) + 2f(y), the constant term is Sum_{k=1}^m a_k. For this to be zero, we have Sum_{k=1}^m a_k =0.

But in addition, for each j ≥1:

Sum_{k=j}^m a_k (-2)^j C(k, j) + 2 a_j =0.

This gives a system of equations for the coefficients a_j.

For j=1:

Sum_{k=1}^m a_k (-2)^1 C(k,1) + 2a_1 =0.

Which is:

Sum_{k=1}^m a_k (-2) k + 2a_1 =0.

But for k=1, term is (-2)(1)a_1. For k≥2, terms are (-2)k a_k. So:

(-2)(1)a_1 + (-2)(2)a_2 + ... + (-2)m a_m + 2a_1 =0.

Simplify:

[ -2a_1 + 2a_1 ] + (-4a_2) + ... + (-2m a_m ) =0.

So -4a_2 -6a_3 - ... -2m a_m =0.

For j=2:

Sum_{k=2}^m a_k (-2)^2 C(k,2) + 2a_2 =0.

That is:

Sum_{k=2}^m a_k 4 C(k,2) + 2a_2 =0.

Similarly, for higher j. This seems complicated, but maybe if we assume that f is of degree m and solve recursively.

Alternatively, suppose that f is a linear combination of certain basis polynomials. But since we have multiple constraints from different n and different a_i's, the only polynomial that can satisfy all these is the zero polynomial.

Alternatively, suppose f is identically zero. Then the sum is zero. But are there other solutions?

Wait, let's test for a specific non-zero polynomial. Suppose f(X) = X(X - 1). Then f(0)=0, and for any x_i's summing to 1, sum f(x_i) = sum x_i(x_i -1) = sum x_i^2 - sum x_i. But sum x_i =1, so total sum is sum x_i^2 -1. For this to be zero for all x_i's summing to 1, sum x_i^2 must always equal 1. But that's not true. For example, if x_i=1 and others zero, sum x_i^2=1. If all x_i=1/n, sum x_i^2 = n*(1/n^2) =1/n. Which is not equal to 1 unless n=1 (but n>2). So for n=3, sum x_i^2=1/3 ≠1. Therefore, sum x_i^2 -1 = -2/3 ≠0. So this polynomial doesn't work.

Alternatively, let's think about symmetric functions. Maybe the sum of f(x_i) is a symmetric function in x_i's. But the condition is that it must vanish for any x_i's summing to 1. If f is linear, we saw it has to be zero. For higher degrees, similar.

Alternatively, maybe f must be a multiple of x(x - 1)(x - c)... but even then, sum over x_i's would not necessarily vanish.

Alternatively, suppose that f is identically zero. Then the condition is satisfied. But are there other polynomials?

Wait, maybe we can use linear algebra. The set of polynomials f with f(0)=0 is a vector space with basis {X, X^2, X^3, ...}. The condition that sum_{k=1}^n f(x_k) =0 for all n>2 and x_k real with sum 1, is equivalent to requiring that for any such x_k, the sum is zero. Therefore, if we can show that the only polynomial in this space that vanishes on all such x_k's is the zero polynomial, then the answer is zero.

But the problem is that the x_k's can be any real numbers summing to 1. So the polynomials in f must satisfy that for any choice of points x_1,...,x_n (sum to 1), the sum of f(x_i) is zero.

Phase 4. TEST CREATION: before<#sep#>You are given a string `sentence` representing a sentence. Your task is to implement a function `count_words(sentence)` that returns the number of distinct words in the sentence. Words are considered distinct if they differ in spelling or case. Punctuation should be ignored, and the comparison should be case-insensitive.

**Input:**
- A single string `sentence` (1 ≤ len(sentence) ≤ 1000) containing letters, spaces, and punctuation marks.

**Output:**
- An integer representing the number of distinct words in the sentence.

**Sample Input:**
```
"Hello, world! Hello, universe."
```

**Sample Output:**
```
3
```

```python
import string

def count_words(sentence):
    """
    Counts the number of distinct words in a given sentence, ignoring case and punctuation.
    
    :param sentence: A string representing the sentence.
    :return: An integer representing the number of distinct words.
    """
    sentence = sentence.lower()
    sentence = sentence.translate(str.maketrans('', '', string.punctuation))
    words = sentence.split()
    distinct_words = set(words)
    return len(distinct_words)
```<#sep#> all n>2 and x_k real with sum 1, is equivalent to requiring that for any such x_k, the sum is zero. Therefore, if we can show that the only polynomial in this space that vanishes on all such x_k's is the zero polynomial, then the answer is zero.

But the problem is that the x_k's can be any real numbers summing to 1. So the polynomials in f must satisfy that for any choice of points x_1,...,x_n (sum to 1), the sum of f(x_i) is zero.

This is similar to requiring that the average of f over any finite set of points summing to 1 is zero. But since polynomials are determined by their values on a sufficiently large set, perhaps this forces f to be identically zero.

Alternatively, consider that for any x ∈ ℝ, we can construct a set of points including x and other points such that their sum is 1. For example, fix x, and set n=3, x1 = x, x2 = 1 - x - t, x3 = t. Then sum is x + (1 - x - t) + t =1. Then sum f(x_i) = f(x) + f(1 - x - t) + f(t) =0. For this to hold for all t ∈ ℝ. Let's take t approaching 0. Then x3 approaches 0, so f(t) ≈ f(0) + f’(0) t + ... But f(0)=0, so f(t) ≈ a_1 t. Similarly, 1 - x - t approaches 1 - x. Then the equation becomes f(x) + f(1 - x) + a_1 t ≈0. As t approaches 0, the term a_1 t must approach zero, which it does, but for the equation to hold for all t, we must have f(x) + f(1 - x) =0 and a_1=0. But if a_1=0, then f is at least quadratic.

So if a_1=0, then f(x) is quadratic or higher. Then f(t) ≈ a_2 t^2. Then sum becomes f(x) + f(1 - x - t) + a_2 t^2 =0. Taking t approaching 0, f(1 - x - t) ≈ f(1 - x) - a_2 (2(1 - x)) t + ... (using the derivative). So the equation becomes f(x) + f(1 - x) - a_2 (2(1 - x)) t + a_2 t^2 ≈0. For this to hold as t approaches 0, the coefficients of each power of t must be zero. Therefore:

1. The constant term: f(x) + f(1 - x) =0.
2. The coefficient of t: -2 a_2 (1 - x) =0 for all x. But this implies that a_2=0.

Similarly, proceeding, this would force all coefficients to be zero.

This suggests that the only solution is the zero polynomial.

Alternatively, using mathematical induction. Suppose f is a polynomial of degree m. We want to show that m must be zero (but f(0)=0, so f is zero). Let's assume f is non-zero and of degree m ≥1.

Take n > m +1. Choose x_1 = x, x_2 = y, x_3 = ... = x_n = (1 - x - y)/(n -2). Then the sum becomes f(x) + f(y) + (n -2) f( (1 - x - y)/(n -2) ) =0.

If we take partial derivatives with respect to x and y, we might derive some conditions. However, this might get complicated.

Alternatively, consider that for any x, we can set n-1 variables to (1 - x)/(n -1) and one variable to x. Then sum f(x) + (n -1) f( (1 - x)/(n -1) ) =0. Let’s denote this equation for different values of n.

For example, take n=3: f(x) + 2 f( (1 -x)/2 ) =0.

For n=4: f(x) + 3 f( (1 -x)/3 ) =0.

For n=5: f(x) + 4 f( (1 -x)/4 ) =0.

Etc.

Each of these must hold for all x.

Suppose f is a non-zero polynomial. Let’s consider the functional equations from different n's.

For n=3: f(x) = -2 f( (1 -x)/2 )

For n=4: f(x) = -3 f( (1 -x)/3 )

For n=5: f(x) = -4 f( (1 -x)/4 )

Etc.

If we apply the n=3 equation recursively, perhaps we can find a contradiction unless f is zero.

For example, from n=3: f(x) = -2 f( (1 -x)/2 ).

Apply this again to f( (1 -x)/2 ):

f( (1 -x)/2 ) = -2 f( (1 - (1 -x)/2 ) /2 ) = -2 f( (1 + x)/4 ).

Therefore, f(x) = -2 * (-2) f( (1 + x)/4 ) = 4 f( (1 + x)/4 ).

Similarly, apply the equation again:

f( (1 + x)/4 ) = -2 f( (1 - (1 + x)/4 ) /2 ) = -2 f( (3 - x)/8 )

So f(x) = 4 * (-2) f( (3 - x)/8 ) = -8 f( (3 - x)/8 )

Continuing this process, we get factors of (-2)^k and the argument becomes (1 - x)/2, then (1 + x)/4, etc., which suggests that f(x) must be zero unless it's an infinite product, but since f is a polynomial, which has finite degree, this recursion must terminate, which can only happen if f is identically zero.

Alternatively, suppose f is of degree m. Then each application of the functional equation reduces the argument's scaling. For example, from f(x) = -2 f( (1 - x)/2 ), if f is degree m, then the left-hand side is degree m, and the right-hand side is also degree m (since scaling x by 1/2 doesn't change the degree). However, the coefficients must satisfy certain conditions. For example, if f(x) = c x^m, then:

c x^m = -2 c ( (1 - x)/2 )^m = -2 c (1 - x)^m / 2^m = -c (1 - x)^m / 2^{m -1}.

Therefore, equating coefficients, unless c=0, we have x^m = - (1 - x)^m / 2^{m -1} for all x, which is impossible. Therefore, c=0.

Therefore, the only solution is the zero polynomial.

Hence, after considering various approaches and functional equations, the only polynomial satisfying all conditions is the zero polynomial.

**Final Answer**
The only such polynomial is the zero polynomial. \boxed{0}
</think>To find all polynomials \( f \in \mathbb{C}[X] \) such that \( f(0) = 0 \) and

\[
\sum_{k=1}^n f\left(\frac{a_k}{a_1 + a_2 + \ldots + a_n}\right) = 0
\]

for all \( a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n \in \mathbb{R} \) with \( n > 2 \) and \( a_1 + a_2 + \ldots + a_n \neq 0 \), we proceed as follows:

1. **Initial Condition**: Since \( f(0) = 0 \), the polynomial \( f \) has no constant term. Therefore, we can write \( f(X) = a_1 X + a_2 X^2 + \cdots + a_m X^m \).

2. **Linear Polynomials**: Suppose \( f(X) = cX \). Then,

\[
\sum_{k=1}^n f\left(\frac{a_k}{S}\right) = \sum_{k=1}^n c \left(\frac{a_k}{S}\right) = c \sum_{k=1}^n \frac{a_k}{S} = c \cdot \frac{S}{S} = c.
\]

Definition of terms used in the problem statement:

A shortest path in an undirected graph is a sequence of vertices (v1, v2, ... , vk) such that vi is adjacent to vi + 1 1 ≤ i < k and the sum of weight <image> is minimized where w(i, j) is the edge weight between i and j. (<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortest_path_problem>)

A prime number is a natural number greater than 1 that has no positive divisors other than 1 and itself. (<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_number>)

A minimum spanning tree (MST) is a subset of the edges of a connected, edge-weighted undirected graph that connects all the vertices together, without any cycles and with the minimum possible total edge weight. (<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minimum_spanning_tree>)

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiple_edges>

<#sep#>You are given a string `s`. Your task is to determine if `s` is a palindrome. A string is considered a palindrome if it reads the same forward and backward, ignoring case, spaces, and punctuation. 

**Input:**
- A single string `s` (1 ≤ |s| ≤ 10^5), where |s| denotes the length of the string. The string may contain uppercase and lowercase letters, digits, spaces, and punctuation marks.

**Output:**
- Return `True` if the string is a palindrome, otherwise return `False`.

**Sample Input:**
```
A man, a plan, a canal, Panama
```

**Sample Output:**
```
True
```

**Sample Input:**
```
racecar
```

**Sample Output:**
```
True
```

**Sample Input:**
```
hello
```

**Sample Output:**
```
False
```

```python
import string

def is_palindrome(s):
    """
    Determines if the given string s is a palindrome, ignoring case, spaces, and punctuation.
    
    :param s: The input string to check.
    :return: True if s is a palindrome, False otherwise.
    """
    cleaned = ''.join(c.lower() for c in s if c.isalnum())
    return cleaned == cleaned[::-1]

# Example usage:
# print(is_palindrome("A man, a plan, a canal, Panama"))  # Should return True
# print(is_palindrome("racecar"))                         # Should return True
# print(is_palindrome("hello"))                           # Should return False
```<#sep#>1 X + a_2 X^2 + \cdots + a_m X^m \).

2. **Linear Polynomials**: Suppose \( f(X) = cX \). Then,

\[
\sum_{k=1}^n f\left(\frac{a_k}{S}\right) = \sum_{k=1}^n c \left(\frac{a_k}{S}\right) = c \sum_{k=1}^n \frac{a_k}{S} = c \cdot \frac{S}{S} = c.
\]

For this to be zero for all \( a_i \), we must have \( c = 0 \). Thus, the only linear polynomial is \( f(X) = 0 \).

3. **Higher Degree Polynomials**: Suppose \( f(X) = cX^k \) for some \( k \geq 2 \). Consider \( a_1 = a_2 = \cdots = a_n = 1 \), then \( S = n \) and

\[
\sum_{k=1}^n f\left(\frac{1}{n}\right) = n \cdot f\left(\frac{1}{n}\right) = n \cdot c \left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^k = c \frac{n}{n^k} = c \frac{1}{n^{k-1}}.
\]

For this to be zero for all \( n \), \( c \) must be zero. Therefore, \( f(X) = 0 \) is the only solution for monomials of degree \( k \geq 2 \).

4. **General Polynomials**: Assume \( f(X) = a_1 X + a_2 X^2 + \cdots + a_m X^m \). Consider the specific case where \( n = 3 \) and \( a_1 = t \), \( a_2 = 1 - t \), \( a_3 = 0 \). Then,

\[
f\left(\frac{t}{1}\right) + f\left(\frac{1-t}{1}\right) + f(0) = f(t) + f(1-t) = 0.
\]

This implies \( f(t) = -f(1-t) \). This antisymmetry about \( t = \frac{1}{2} \) suggests that \( f \) must be zero at specific points. To generalize, consider \( n = 4 \) and set \( a_1 = t \), \( a_2 = s \), \( a_3 = 1 - t - s \), \( a_4 = 0 \). Then,

\[
f(t) + f(s) + f(1-t-s) = 0.
\]

By choosing specific values for \( t \) and \( s \), we can derive that each coefficient \( a_i \) must be zero. For instance, setting \( t = s = \frac{1}{3} \) and \( a_3 = \frac{1}{3} \) leads to

\[
f\left(\frac{1}{3}\right) + f\left(\frac{1}{3}\right) + f\left(\frac{1}{3}\right) = 3 f\left(\frac{1}{3}\right) = 0 \implies f\left(\frac{1}{3}\right) = 0.
\]

By continuing this process for various values and degrees, we conclude that all coefficients \( a_i \) must be zero.

Therefore, the only polynomial \( f \) that satisfies the given conditions is the zero polynomial.

    37	2. create `spectree.SpecTree` instance with the web framework name you are using, like `api = SpecTree('flask')`
    38	3. `api.validate` decorate the route with
    39	   * `query`
    40	   * `json`
    41	   * `headers`
    42	   * `cookies`
    43	   * `resp`
    44	   * `tags`
    45	4. access these data with `context(query, json, headers, cookies)` (of course, you can access these from the original place where the framework offered)
    46	   * flask: `request.context`
    47	   * falcon: `req.context`
    48	   * starlette: `request.context`
    49	5. register to the web application `api.register(app)`
    50	6. check the document at URL location `/apidoc/redoc` or `/apidoc/swagger`
    51	
    52	If the request doesn't pass the validation, it will return a 422 with JSON error message(ctx, loc, msg, type).
    53	
    54	## How To
    55	
    56	> How to add summary and description to endpoints?
    57	
    58	Just add docs to the endpoint function. The 1st line is the summary, and the rest is the description for this endpoint.
    59	
    60	> How to add description to parameters?
    61	
    62	Check the [pydantic](https://pydantic-docs.helpmanual.io/usage/schema/) document about description in `Field`.
    63	
    64	> Any config I can change?
    65	
    66	Of course. Check the [config](https://spectree.readthedocs.io/en/latest/config.html) document.
    67	
    68	You can update the config when init the spectree like: 
    69	
    70	```py
    71	SpecTree('flask', title='Demo API', version='v1.0', path='doc')
    72	```
    73	
    74	> What is `Response` and how to use it?
    75	
    76	To build a response for the endpoint, you need to declare the status code with format `HTTP_{code}` and corresponding data (optional).
    77	
    78	```py
    79	Response(HTTP_200=None, HTTP_403=ForbidModel)
    80	Response('HTTP_200') # equals to Response(HTTP_200=None)
    81	```
    82	
    83	> What should I return when I'm using the library?
    84	
    85	No need to change anything. Just return what the framework required.
    86	
    87	> How to logging when the validation failed?
    88	
    89	Validation errors are logged with INFO level. Details are passed into `extra`. Check the [falcon example](examples/falcon_demo.py) for details.
    90	
    91	> How can I write a customized plugin for another backend framework?
    92	
    93	Inherit `spectree.plugins.base.BasePlugin` and implement the functions you need. After that, init like `api = SpecTree(backend=MyCustomizedPlugin)`.
    94	
    95	> How can I change the response when there is a validation error? Can I record some metrics?
    96	
    97	This library provides `before` and `after` hooks to do these. Check the [doc](https://spectree.readthedocs.io/en/latest) or the [test case](tests/test_plugin_flask.py). You can change the handlers for SpecTree or for a specific endpoint validation.
    98	
    99	## Demo
   100	
   101	Try it with `http post :8000/api/user name=alice age=18`. (if you are using `httpie`)
   102	
   103	### Flask
   104	
   105	```py
   106	from flask import Flask, request, jsonify
   107	from pydantic import BaseModel, Field, constr
   108	from spectree import SpecTree, Response
   109	
   110	
   111	class Profile(BaseModel):
   112	    name: constr(min_length=2, max_length=40) # Constrained Str
   113	    age: int = Field(
   114	        ...,
   115	        gt=0,
   116	        lt=150,
   117	        description='user age(Human)'
   118	    )
   119	
   120	    class Config:
   121	        schema_extra = {
   122	            # provide an example
   123	            'example': {
   124	                'name': 'very_important_user',
   125	                'age': 42,
   126	            }
   127	        }
   128	
   129	
   130	class Message(BaseModel):
   131	    text: str
   132	
   133	
   134	app = Flask(__name__)
   135	api = SpecTree('flask')
   136	
   137	
   138	@app.route('/api/user', methods=['POST'])
   139	@api.validate(json=Profile, resp=Response(HTTP_200=Message, HTTP_403=None), tags=['api'])
   140	def user_profile():
   141	    """
   142	    verify user profile (summary of this endpoint)
   143	
   144	    user's name, user's age, ... (long description)
   145	    """
   146	    print(request.context.json) # or `request.json`
   147	    return jsonify(text='it works')
   148	
   149	
   150	if __name__ == "__main__":
   151	    api.register(app) # if you don't register in api init step
   152	    app.run(port=8000)
   153	
   154	```
   155	
   156	### Falcon
   157	
   158	```py
   159	import falcon
   160	from wsgiref import simple_server
   161	from pydantic import BaseModel, Field, constr
   162	from spectree import SpecTree, Response
   163	
   164	
   165	class Profile(BaseModel):
   166	    name: constr(min_length=2, max_length=40)  # Constrained Str
   167	    age: int = Field(
   168	        ...,
   169	        gt=0,
   170	        lt=150,
   171	        description='user age(Human)'
   172	    )
   173	
   174	
   175	class Message(BaseModel):
   176	    text: str
   177	
   178	
   179	api = SpecTree('falcon')
   180	
   181	
   182	class UserProfile:
   183	    @api.validate(json=Profile, resp=Response(HTTP_200=Message, HTTP_403=None), tags=['api'])
   184	    def on_post(self, req, resp):
   185	        """
   186	        verify user profile (summary of this endpoint)
   187	
   188	        user's name, user's age, ... (long description)
   189	        """
   190	        print(req.context.json)  # or `req.media`
   191	        resp.media = {'text': 'it works'}
   192	
   193	
   194	if __name__ == "__main__":
   195	    app = falcon.API()
   196	    app.add_route('/api/user', UserProfile())
   197	    api.register(app)
   198	
   199	    httpd = simple_server.make_server('localhost', 8000, app)
   200	    httpd.serve_forever()
   201	
   202	```
   203	
   204	### Starlette
   205	
   206	```py
   207	import uvicorn
   208	from starlette.applications import Starlette
   209	from starlette.routing import Route, Mount
   210	from starlette.responses import JSONResponse
   211	from pydantic import BaseModel, Field, constr
   212	from spectree import SpecTree, Response
   213	
   214	
   215	class Profile(BaseModel):
  <#sep#>You are given a positive integer `n`. Your task is to write a function `find_largest_prime_factor(n)` that returns the largest prime factor of `n`. If `n` itself is a prime number, return `n`.

**Constraints:**
- `2 ≤ n ≤ 10^12`

**Sample Input:**
```
100
```

**Sample Output:**
```
5
```

**Sample Input:**
```
13195
```

**Sample Output:**
```
29
```

```python
def find_largest_prime_factor(n):
    """
    Returns the largest prime factor of a given positive integer n.
    If n is a prime number, returns n.
    
    :param n: int - the positive integer to find the largest prime factor of
    :return: int - the largest prime factor of n
    """
    if n <= 1:
        raise ValueError("Input should be a positive integer greater than 1.")
    
    def is_prime(num):
        """
        Checks if a number is prime.
        
        :param num: int - the number to check for primality
        :return: bool - True if num is prime, False otherwise
        """
        if num <= 1:
            return False
        if num <= 3:
            return True
        if num % 2 == 0 or num % 3 == 0:
            return False
        i = 5
        while i * i <= num:
            if num % i == 0 or num % (i + 2) == 0:
                return False
            i += 6
        return True
    
    if is_prime(n):
        return n
    
    largest_factor = None
    while n % 2 == 0:
        largest_factor = 2
        n //= 2
    
    for i in range(3, int(n**0.5) + 1, 2):
        while n % i == 0:
            largest_factor = i
            n //= i
    
    if n > 2:
        largest_factor = n
    
    return largest_factor
```<#sep#>Find the kernel (kerB) and the image (ImB) of the transformation \( B \) defined by \( B(x, y) = (x - y, x - y) \).

</think>To find the kernel (kerB) and the image (ImB) of the transformation \( B \) defined by \( B(x, y) = (x - y, x - y) \), we proceed as follows:

### Kernel (kerB)
The kernel of \( B \) is the set of all vectors \((x, y)\) such that \( B(x, y) = (0, 0) \).

1. Set up the equation for the kernel:
   \[
   B(x, y) = (x - y, x - y) = (0, 0)
   \]
2. This gives us the equation:
   \[
   x - y = 0
   \]
3. Solving for \( y \), we get:
   \[
   y = x
   \]
4. Therefore, the kernel consists of all vectors of the form \((x, x)\) where \( x \) is any real number. Formally, we write:
   \[
   \text{kerB} = \{(x, x) \mid x \in \mathbb{R}\}
   \]

### Image (ImB)
The image of \( B \) is the set of all possible outputs of the transformation \( B \).

1. Consider the general form of the transformation:
   \[
   B(x, y) = (x - y, x - y)
   \]
2. Notice that both components of the output vector are the same. Let \( a = x - y \). Then the output vector is:
   \[
   (a, a)
   \]
3. Since \( a \) can be any real number (as \( x \) and \( y \) can vary over all real numbers), the image consists of all vectors of the form \((a, a)\) where \( a \) is any real number. Formally, we write:
   \[
   \text{ImB} = \{(a, a) \mid a \in \mathbb{R}\}
   \]

### Final Answers
\[
\boxed{\text{kerB} = \{(x, x) \mid x \in \mathbb{R}\}}
\]
\[
\boxed{\text{ImB} = \{(a, a) \mid a \in \mathbb{R}\}}
\]

   204	### Starlette
   205	
   206	```py
   207	import uvicorn
   208	from starlette.applications import Starlette
   209	from starlette.routing import Route, Mount
   210	from starlette.responses import JSONResponse
   211	from pydantic import BaseModel, Field, constr
   212	from spectree import SpecTree, Response
   213	
   214	
   215	class Profile(BaseModel):
   216	    name: constr(min_length=2, max_length=40)  # Constrained Str
   217	    age: int = Field(
   218	        ...,
   219	        gt=0,
   220	        lt=150,
   221	        description='user age(Human)'
   222	    )
   223	
   224	
   225	class Message(BaseModel):
   226	    text: str
   227	
   228	
   229	api = SpecTree('starlette')
   230	
   231	
   232	@api.validate(json=Profile, resp=Response(HTTP_200=Message, HTTP_403=None), tags=['api'])
   233	async def user_profile(request):
   234	    """
   235	    verify user profile (summary of this endpoint)
   236	
   237	    user's name, user's age, ... (long description)
   238	    """
   239	    print(request.context.json)  # or await request.json()
   240	    return JSONResponse({'text': 'it works'})
   241	
   242	
   243	if __name__ == "__main__":
   244	    app = Starlette(routes=[
   245	        Mount('api', routes=[
   246	            Route('/user', user_profile, methods=['POST']),
   247	        ])
   248	    ])
   249	    api.register(app)
   250	
   251	    uvicorn.run(app)
   252	
   253	```
   254	
   255	## FAQ
   256	
   257	> ValidationError: missing field for headers
   258	
   259	The HTTP headers' keys in Flask are capitalized, in Falcon are upper cases, in Starlette are lower cases.
   260	You can use [`pydantic.root_validators(pre=True)`](https://pydantic-docs.helpmanual.io/usage/validators/#root-validators) to change all the keys into lower cases or upper cases.
   261	
   262	> ValidationError: value is not a valid list for query
   263	
   264	Since there is no standard for HTTP query with multiple values, it's hard to find the way to handle this for different web frameworks. So I suggest not to use list type in query until I find a suitable way to fix it.
   265

  /testbed/spectree/spec.py:133: PydanticDeprecatedSince20: The `<#sep#>You are tasked with preprocessing a large corpus of articles for a text summarization project. Your goal is to implement a function `preprocess_text(text)` that takes a string `text` as input and returns a list of words after performing the following preprocessing steps:

1. Convert all characters to lowercase.
2. Remove all punctuation marks.
3. Replace all sequences of digits with the token 'NUM'.
4. Tokenize the text into words.
5. Remove all English stopwords.

**Input:**
- A single string `text` (1 ≤ len(text) ≤ 10^5).

**Output:**
- A list of preprocessed words.

**Sample Input:**
```
"Python 3.8 is a great language! It's widely used in data science and machine learning."
```

**Sample Output:**
```
['python', 'num', 'great', 'language', 'widely', 'used', 'data', 'science', 'machine', 'learning']
```

```python
import re
from nltk.corpus import stopwords

def preprocess_text(text):
    """
    Preprocesses the input text by converting it to lowercase, removing punctuation,
    replacing numbers with 'NUM', tokenizing into words, and removing stopwords.

    Parameters:
    text (str): The input text to preprocess.

    Returns:
    list: A list of preprocessed words.
    """
    # Convert to lowercase
    text = text.lower()
    
    # Remove all punctuation
    text = re.sub(r'[^\w\s]', '', text)
    
    # Replace all numbers with 'NUM'
    text = re.sub(r'\d+', 'NUM', text)
    
    # Tokenize the text into words
    words = text.split()
    
    # Remove all stopwords
    stop_words = set(stopwords.words('english'))
    filtered_words = [word for word in words if word not in stop_words]
    
    return filtered_words

Your task is to implement a function `parse_json(file_path)` that reads this JSON file and returns a list of dictionaries, where each dictionary corresponds to an employee and contains their respective attributes as keys.

**Input:**
- A string `file_path` representing the path to the JSON file `data.json`.

**Output:**
- A list of dictionaries, where each dictionary contains the keys `id`, `name`, `department`, and `email` with their corresponding values.

**Sample Input:**
```json
[
    {"id": 1, "name": "John Doe", "department": "Engineering", "email": "john.doe@example.com"},
    {"id": 2, "name": "Jane Smith", "department": "Marketing", "email": "jane.smith@example.com"}
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
[
    {'id': 1, 'name': 'John Doe', 'department': 'Engineering', 'email': 'john.doe@example.com'},
    {'id': 2, 'name': 'Jane Smith', 'department': 'Marketing', 'email': 'jane.smith@example.com'}
]
```

```python
import json

def parse_json(file_path):
    """
    Reads a JSON file containing employee records and returns a list of dictionaries.

    Parameters:
    file_path (str): The path to the JSON file.

    Returns:
    list: A list of dictionaries, each representing an employee with their attributes.
    """
    with open(file_path, 'r') as file:
        employees = json.load(file)
    return employees

# Example usage:
# employee_list = parse_json('data.json')
# print(employee_list)
